People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hey, doc. Nice to meet you. Look, I'm here because... well, for the past year or so, I just can't seem to stay on track. The absolute smallest thing just yanks my attention away before I can even stop it. Like a phone buzz, or somebody walking past my office. I got these floor-to-ceiling glass walls put in the office last year, actually. Thought it would make the company look more modern, you know? Open concept is huge right now, even though it cost an absolute fortune to install. 

But yeah, even just a random thought about my in-laws pulls me completely off what I'm doing. Which, speaking of my in-laws, my wife's father just bought this massive RV. I mean, the guy is pushing seventy and he thinks he's gonna drive a forty-foot bus across the country? He can barely back out of his own driveway without clipping the garbage cans. I told him he should just rent a place in Florida, but nobody listens to me about that stuff. He's got this whole route planned out through the Midwest. 

Anyway... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, why I'm here. Yeah, so I just get pulled off what I'm doing really easily lately.

I wouldn't say I'm worrying. Honestly, what's there to worry about? I dropped out in eleventh grade and still made it to CEO, so I'm pretty good at handling whatever gets thrown at me. It's more that my brain just... goes somewhere else.

Well, looking back... yeah, it's definitely not new. I mean, I've always been like this, even as a kid. My teachers were constantly moving my seat because I talked too much or I'd just get up without asking. And I was always blurting out answers before they even finished the question. Actually, I remember my fifth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive beehive hairdo, like something straight out of the sixties. I don't know how she kept it up like that, probably went through a can of hairspray a week. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, which was right by the radiator, so I was always sweating. 

My mom tried to talk to the principal about it once, because I kept coming home complaining about how hot it was. My mom was pretty intense when she wanted to be. She used to run this local bakery, made the best sourdough in town. She'd get up at like three in the morning every day to start the dough. I tried to help her out a few times when I was a teenager, but standing in one spot kneading bread for an hour? Forget about it. 

But yeah, anyway, the school thing... yeah, I've always had trouble sitting still and keeping my mouth shut. It's not just a recent thing.

Well, yeah, like I was saying, it goes way back. Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. 

Actually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. 

Anyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. 

But yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.

Like, in meetings, I'm always bouncing my knee or shifting around in my chair. Actually, we just upgraded all the conference room chairs last month. I got these imported ergonomic mesh ones—cost an absolute arm and a leg, but my chiropractor said I needed better lumbar support. I threw my lower back out last summer jet skiing, which was a total nightmare because I had to spend three days flat on my back in the cabin while everyone else was out on the water. The jet ski is one of those stand-up ones, way faster than the sit-down kind, but you really feel it in your knees and back if you hit a wake wrong. \n\nBut yeah, what was I saying? Oh, right, the meetings. Yeah, I'm always shifting around in those expensive chairs, or I'm sitting there clicking a pen myself. I just can't seem to stay frozen in one spot.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these attention and restlessness issues started about a year ago. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. 

Actually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. 

But yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.

Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. \n\nActually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. \n\nAnyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\n[Last Question]\nBack when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? Like, I wasn't failing out or anything. I mean, I dropped out eventually, but that was different. Before that, my grades were... fine, I guess. Nothing spectacular, but nobody was calling home saying I was ruining my life. 

Actually, my history teacher in middle school, Mr. Davies, he was the one who really got me into World War II history. He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. I found this incredible piece at an estate sale—a compass that supposedly belonged to a paratrooper in the 101st Airborne. The guy selling it had all these documents, but you never really know with that stuff, right? You have to get it authenticated, which is a whole process. There's this guy in Chicago I send pictures to, he's like the foremost expert on WWII field gear. 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, school. Yeah, I don't remember anyone being super worried about my future or anything because of how I was acting. Just the usual telling me to sit down and be quiet.

I tried to help her out a few times when I was a teenager, but standing in one spot kneading bread for an hour? Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\nDoctor: Back when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. No, no, nothing like that. I never got held back or put in any special classes or anything. I mean, I was always in the regular classes with everyone else. 

Actually, speaking of regular classes, I remember my fifth-grade classroom had this huge terrarium in the back. Our teacher, Mrs. Gable, she kept a bearded dragon in there. His name was Spike. I used to volunteer to feed him crickets during recess because, honestly, I just liked watching him hunt. Crickets are surprisingly fast, but Spike had this way of just sitting perfectly still until they got close enough, and then—bam. Gone. But my wife said absolutely not, she draws the line at the dogs. Plus, I don't think my assistant would be thrilled about keeping live insects in the breakroom fridge. But yeah, no special classes or repeating grades for me. Just regular school until I left.

I just can't seem to stay frozen in one spot.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these attention and restlessness issues started about a year ago. But thinking back to when you were younger—in school, or as a teenager—did you have similar problems with focusing or sitting still back then, or is this something that's pretty new to you?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back... yeah, it's definitely not new. I mean, I've always been like this, even as a kid. My teachers were constantly moving my seat because I talked too much or I'd just get up without asking. And I was always blurting out answers before they even finished the question. Actually, I remember my fifth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive beehive hairdo, like something straight out of the sixties. I don't know how she kept it up like that, probably went through a can of hairspray a week. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, which was right by the radiator, so I was always sweating. \n\nMy mom tried to talk to the principal about it once, because I kept coming home complaining about how hot it was. My mom was pretty intense when she wanted to be. She used to run this local bakery, made the best sourdough in town. She'd get up at like three in the morning every day to start the dough. I tried to help her out a few times when I was a teenager, but standing in one spot kneading bread for an hour? Forget about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the school thing... yeah, I've always had trouble sitting still and keeping my mouth shut. It's not just a recent thing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before fifth grade even, do you remember having these same kinds of problems—trouble paying attention in class or difficulty sitting still—or did those issues start later on?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it goes way back. Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. \n\nActually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. \n\nAnyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\nDoctor: Back when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? Like, I wasn't failing out or anything. I mean, I dropped out eventually, but that was different. Before that, my grades were... fine, I guess. Nothing spectacular, but nobody was calling home saying I was ruining my life. \n\nActually, my history teacher in middle school, Mr. Davies, he was the one who really got me into World War II history. He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. I found this incredible piece at an estate sale—a compass that supposedly belonged to a paratrooper in the 101st Airborne. The guy selling it had all these documents, but you never really know with that stuff, right? You have to get it authenticated, which is a whole process. There's this guy in Chicago I send pictures to, he's like the foremost expert on WWII field gear. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, school. Yeah, I don't remember anyone being super worried about my future or anything because of how I was acting. Just the usual telling me to sit down and be quiet.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did your teachers ever hold you back a grade, or did you ever need to repeat a class, or get put in a special classroom for kids who were struggling academically?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I never got held back or put in any special classes or anything. I mean, I was always in the regular classes with everyone else. \n\nActually, speaking of regular classes, I remember my fifth-grade classroom had this huge terrarium in the back. Our teacher, Mrs. Gable, she kept a bearded dragon in there. His name was Spike. I used to volunteer to feed him crickets during recess because, honestly, I just liked watching him hunt. Crickets are surprisingly fast, but Spike had this way of just sitting perfectly still until they got close enough, and then—bam. Gone. I actually thought about getting a reptile for my office a while back, maybe a chameleon. They're fascinating, the way their eyes move independently. But my wife said absolutely not, she draws the line at the dogs. Plus, I don't think my assistant would be thrilled about keeping live insects in the breakroom fridge. She already complains about the smell of my protein shakes if I leave the shaker bottle in the sink too long. \n\nBut yeah, no special classes or repeating grades for me. Just regular school until I left.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Suspended? No, never. Never expelled either. And I definitely didn't do summer school. I couldn't have handled sitting in a classroom during the summer anyway. 

Actually, summer was always my favorite time because my dad would take me out to his buddy's auto shop. That's where I first learned how to rebuild an engine. His friend had this 1969 Mustang Mach 1—candy apple red. Gorgeous car. We spent an entire July just stripping down the carburetor and putting it back together. I still love the smell of motor oil and gasoline, you know? It's weird, but it's comforting. I was actually looking at classic cars online the other night instead of sleeping. Found a pristine '72 Chevelle SS down in Texas, but the guy wants way too much for it. People think just because it has matching numbers they can charge whatever they want, but the market for classic muscle cars is actually softening a bit right now. 

But anyway... what did you ask? Oh, right, catching up academically. No, I never had to take any special classes or anything like that. Just went along normally until I decided I'd had enough of school.

Honestly, I'm not really sure if it was one or the other. I don't remember the work being impossible or anything, but maybe I just didn't care enough to find out? I just knew I didn't want to be sitting at a desk all day. I wanted to be out actually doing things, making money.

And I was always blurting out answers before they even finished the question. Actually, I remember my fifth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive beehive hairdo, like something straight out of the sixties. I don't know how she kept it up like that, probably went through a can of hairspray a week. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, which was right by the radiator, so I was always sweating. \n\nMy mom tried to talk to the principal about it once, because I kept coming home complaining about how hot it was. My mom was pretty intense when she wanted to be. She used to run this local bakery, made the best sourdough in town. She'd get up at like three in the morning every day to start the dough. I tried to help her out a few times when I was a teenager, but standing in one spot kneading bread for an hour? Forget about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the school thing... yeah, I've always had trouble sitting still and keeping my mouth shut. It's not just a recent thing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before fifth grade even, do you remember having these same kinds of problems—trouble paying attention in class or difficulty sitting still—or did those issues start later on?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it goes way back. Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. \n\nActually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. \n\nAnyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\nDoctor: Back when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? Like, I wasn't failing out or anything. I mean, I dropped out eventually, but that was different. Before that, my grades were... fine, I guess. Nothing spectacular, but nobody was calling home saying I was ruining my life. \n\nActually, my history teacher in middle school, Mr. Davies, he was the one who really got me into World War II history. He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. I found this incredible piece at an estate sale—a compass that supposedly belonged to a paratrooper in the 101st Airborne. The guy selling it had all these documents, but you never really know with that stuff, right? You have to get it authenticated, which is a whole process. There's this guy in Chicago I send pictures to, he's like the foremost expert on WWII field gear. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, school. Yeah, I don't remember anyone being super worried about my future or anything because of how I was acting. Just the usual telling me to sit down and be quiet.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did your teachers ever hold you back a grade, or did you ever need to repeat a class, or get put in a special classroom for kids who were struggling academically?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I never got held back or put in any special classes or anything. I mean, I was always in the regular classes with everyone else. \n\nActually, speaking of regular classes, I remember my fifth-grade classroom had this huge terrarium in the back. Our teacher, Mrs. Gable, she kept a bearded dragon in there. His name was Spike. I used to volunteer to feed him crickets during recess because, honestly, I just liked watching him hunt. Crickets are surprisingly fast, but Spike had this way of just sitting perfectly still until they got close enough, and then—bam. Gone. I actually thought about getting a reptile for my office a while back, maybe a chameleon. They're fascinating, the way their eyes move independently. But my wife said absolutely not, she draws the line at the dogs. Plus, I don't think my assistant would be thrilled about keeping live insects in the breakroom fridge. She already complains about the smell of my protein shakes if I leave the shaker bottle in the sink too long. \n\nBut yeah, no special classes or repeating grades for me. Just regular school until I left.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever suspended or expelled from school, or did you ever have to go to summer school or take any special classes to catch up academically?\n\nPatient: Suspended? No, never. Never expelled either. And I definitely didn't do summer school. I couldn't have handled sitting in a classroom during the summer anyway. \n\nActually, summer was always my favorite time because my dad would take me out to his buddy's auto shop. That's where I first learned how to rebuild an engine. His friend had this 1969 Mustang Mach 1—candy apple red. Gorgeous car. We spent an entire July just stripping down the carburetor and putting it back together. I still love the smell of motor oil and gasoline, you know? It's weird, but it's comforting. I was actually looking at classic cars online the other night instead of sleeping. Found a pristine '72 Chevelle SS down in Texas, but the guy wants way too much for it. People think just because it has matching numbers they can charge whatever they want, but the market for classic muscle cars is actually softening a bit right now. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask? Oh, right, catching up academically. No, I never had to take any special classes or anything like that. Just went along normally until I decided I'd had enough of school.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you dropped out in eleventh grade. Before that happened, were you actually struggling to keep up with the schoolwork itself, or was it more that you just didn't want to be there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure if it was one or the other. I don't remember the work being impossible or anything, but maybe I just didn't care enough to find out? I just knew I didn't want to be sitting at a desk all day. I wanted to be out actually doing things, making money. \n\nRight after I left, I got a job installing car stereos at this local place off Route 9. Audio Express, I think it was called. Man, the things people would ask us to do to their cars... We had this one guy bring in a completely beat-up '98 Civic, and he wanted two massive 12-inch subwoofers crammed into the trunk. We had to use so much of that sound-deadening mat to keep the trunk lid from rattling right off the hinges, and even then, his license plate just vibrated like crazy. I learned a ton about electrical wiring there, though. You have to be super careful running the main power wire from the battery through the firewall, because if it rubs against the bare metal and shorts out... well, you're gonna have a fire on your hands. \n\nI actually still install my own audio equipment in my cars today. I just put a custom system in my truck last weekend. Took me like six hours, but the sound quality is just unbelievable compared to the factory speakers. The dealership always tries to sell you on their premium audio packages, but it's such a rip-off for what you actually get... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about eleventh grade?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't really remember it being hard to learn, honestly. I think I understood things fine when I actually paid attention. The problem was just sitting there while the teacher droned on and on. 

I actually had this one teacher in third grade, Mr. Peterson. He used to bring his guitar to class and play songs to help us remember multiplication tables. He had this vintage Martin acoustic, absolutely beautiful instrument. The resonance on those old Martins is just incredible, the wood ages in a way that gives it such a warm tone. He's actually getting pretty good, started a little garage band with his friends. They play mostly 90s grunge covers, which I think is hilarious because they weren't even born when Nirvana was big. It's funny how music cycles back around like that, isn't it? Like vinyl records making a comeback. I saw a turntable at Best Buy the other day for like five hundred bucks...

Sorry, what were we talking about? Elementary school work? Yeah, I don't remember it being particularly confusing or anything.

Something about eleventh grade?\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if you were actually able to understand the lessons and do the work okay, or did you find the schoolwork itself confusing or hard to learn?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember it being hard to learn, honestly. I think I understood things fine when I actually paid attention. The problem was just sitting there while the teacher droned on and on. \n\nI actually had this one teacher in third grade, Mr. Peterson. He used to bring his guitar to class and play songs to help us remember multiplication tables. He had this vintage Martin acoustic, absolutely beautiful instrument. The resonance on those old Martins is just incredible, the wood ages in a way that gives it such a warm tone. I tried picking up guitar a few years ago, bought myself a nice Taylor, thinking I'd just learn a few chords to unwind after work. But man, building up those calluses on your fingers is brutal. And trying to find the time to practice consistently when you're running a company? Forget about it. I ended up giving the guitar to my nephew for his birthday. He's actually getting pretty good, started a little garage band with his friends. They play mostly 90s grunge covers, which I think is hilarious because they weren't even born when Nirvana was big. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall. The business is running, things are getting done. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like, my wife is losing trust in me because family plans just don't go the way they should, you know? And at work, people are losing their patience with me during meetings or when I'm supposed to be mentoring the younger guys. 

Speaking of mentoring, we just hired this kid straight out of college, engineering degree. Smart kid, but he doesn't know the first thing about actual practical application. He tried to redesign one of our standard manufacturing processes on his second day. I had to sit him down and explain that just because something looks more efficient on CAD software doesn't mean it translates to the factory floor. The software doesn't account for the fact that the forklift needs a wider turning radius when it's fully loaded, or that the hydraulic presses have a ten-second reset time. I actually love those old hydraulic presses we have. They're these massive, hulking machines from the 80s, built like absolute tanks. We had a guy from the manufacturer come out to service one last month, and he said they don't even make the replacement parts anymore. We have to custom machine our own seals for the pistons now. I actually went down to the shop floor and helped the machinist turn a new seal on the lathe. There's something really satisfying about watching metal shavings peel off a block of steel, you know? It's almost hypnotic. I could watch a lathe run all day. I actually watched like three hours of machining videos on YouTube last night...

Wait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking if things are actually preventing me from doing what I need to do. Yeah, like I said, I'm getting things done, but it's definitely causing friction with my wife and the guys at work.

Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. \n\nActually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. \n\nAnyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\nDoctor: Back when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? Like, I wasn't failing out or anything. I mean, I dropped out eventually, but that was different. Before that, my grades were... fine, I guess. Nothing spectacular, but nobody was calling home saying I was ruining my life. \n\nActually, my history teacher in middle school, Mr. Davies, he was the one who really got me into World War II history. He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. I found this incredible piece at an estate sale—a compass that supposedly belonged to a paratrooper in the 101st Airborne. The guy selling it had all these documents, but you never really know with that stuff, right? You have to get it authenticated, which is a whole process. There's this guy in Chicago I send pictures to, he's like the foremost expert on WWII field gear. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, school. Yeah, I don't remember anyone being super worried about my future or anything because of how I was acting. Just the usual telling me to sit down and be quiet.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did your teachers ever hold you back a grade, or did you ever need to repeat a class, or get put in a special classroom for kids who were struggling academically?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I never got held back or put in any special classes or anything. I mean, I was always in the regular classes with everyone else. \n\nActually, speaking of regular classes, I remember my fifth-grade classroom had this huge terrarium in the back. Our teacher, Mrs. Gable, she kept a bearded dragon in there. His name was Spike. I used to volunteer to feed him crickets during recess because, honestly, I just liked watching him hunt. Crickets are surprisingly fast, but Spike had this way of just sitting perfectly still until they got close enough, and then—bam. Gone. I actually thought about getting a reptile for my office a while back, maybe a chameleon. They're fascinating, the way their eyes move independently. But my wife said absolutely not, she draws the line at the dogs. Plus, I don't think my assistant would be thrilled about keeping live insects in the breakroom fridge. She already complains about the smell of my protein shakes if I leave the shaker bottle in the sink too long. \n\nBut yeah, no special classes or repeating grades for me. Just regular school until I left.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever suspended or expelled from school, or did you ever have to go to summer school or take any special classes to catch up academically?\n\nPatient: Suspended? No, never. Never expelled either. And I definitely didn't do summer school. I couldn't have handled sitting in a classroom during the summer anyway. \n\nActually, summer was always my favorite time because my dad would take me out to his buddy's auto shop. That's where I first learned how to rebuild an engine. His friend had this 1969 Mustang Mach 1—candy apple red. Gorgeous car. We spent an entire July just stripping down the carburetor and putting it back together. I still love the smell of motor oil and gasoline, you know? It's weird, but it's comforting. I was actually looking at classic cars online the other night instead of sleeping. Found a pristine '72 Chevelle SS down in Texas, but the guy wants way too much for it. People think just because it has matching numbers they can charge whatever they want, but the market for classic muscle cars is actually softening a bit right now. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask? Oh, right, catching up academically. No, I never had to take any special classes or anything like that. Just went along normally until I decided I'd had enough of school.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you dropped out in eleventh grade. Before that happened, were you actually struggling to keep up with the schoolwork itself, or was it more that you just didn't want to be there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure if it was one or the other. I don't remember the work being impossible or anything, but maybe I just didn't care enough to find out? I just knew I didn't want to be sitting at a desk all day. I wanted to be out actually doing things, making money. \n\nRight after I left, I got a job installing car stereos at this local place off Route 9. Audio Express, I think it was called. Man, the things people would ask us to do to their cars... We had this one guy bring in a completely beat-up '98 Civic, and he wanted two massive 12-inch subwoofers crammed into the trunk. We had to use so much of that sound-deadening mat to keep the trunk lid from rattling right off the hinges, and even then, his license plate just vibrated like crazy. I learned a ton about electrical wiring there, though. You have to be super careful running the main power wire from the battery through the firewall, because if it rubs against the bare metal and shorts out... well, you're gonna have a fire on your hands. \n\nI actually still install my own audio equipment in my cars today. I just put a custom system in my truck last weekend. Took me like six hours, but the sound quality is just unbelievable compared to the factory speakers. The dealership always tries to sell you on their premium audio packages, but it's such a rip-off for what you actually get... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about eleventh grade?\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if you were actually able to understand the lessons and do the work okay, or did you find the schoolwork itself confusing or hard to learn?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember it being hard to learn, honestly. I think I understood things fine when I actually paid attention. The problem was just sitting there while the teacher droned on and on. \n\nI actually had this one teacher in third grade, Mr. Peterson. He used to bring his guitar to class and play songs to help us remember multiplication tables. He had this vintage Martin acoustic, absolutely beautiful instrument. The resonance on those old Martins is just incredible, the wood ages in a way that gives it such a warm tone. I tried picking up guitar a few years ago, bought myself a nice Taylor, thinking I'd just learn a few chords to unwind after work. But man, building up those calluses on your fingers is brutal. And trying to find the time to practice consistently when you're running a company? Forget about it. I ended up giving the guitar to my nephew for his birthday. He's actually getting pretty good, started a little garage band with his friends. They play mostly 90s grunge covers, which I think is hilarious because they weren't even born when Nirvana was big. It's funny how music cycles back around like that, isn't it? Like vinyl records making a comeback. I saw a turntable at Best Buy the other day for like five hundred bucks...\n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Elementary school work? Yeah, I don't remember it being particularly confusing or anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things are getting harder to manage at work and at home with your wife. Looking back over the past year or so, would you say these attention and restlessness problems have actually prevented you from doing things you needed to do, or have they mostly been annoying but you've still been able to get things done?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall. The business is running, things are getting done. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like, my wife is losing trust in me because family plans just don't go the way they should, you know? And at work, people are losing their patience with me during meetings or when I'm supposed to be mentoring the younger guys. \n\nSpeaking of mentoring, we just hired this kid straight out of college, engineering degree. Smart kid, but he doesn't know the first thing about actual practical application. He tried to redesign one of our standard manufacturing processes on his second day. I had to sit him down and explain that just because something looks more efficient on CAD software doesn't mean it translates to the factory floor. The software doesn't account for the fact that the forklift needs a wider turning radius when it's fully loaded, or that the hydraulic presses have a ten-second reset time. I actually love those old hydraulic presses we have. They're these massive, hulking machines from the 80s, built like absolute tanks. We had a guy from the manufacturer come out to service one last month, and he said they don't even make the replacement parts anymore. We have to custom machine our own seals for the pistons now. I actually went down to the shop floor and helped the machinist turn a new seal on the lathe. There's something really satisfying about watching metal shavings peel off a block of steel, you know? It's almost hypnotic. I could watch a lathe run all day. I actually watched like three hours of machining videos on YouTube last night...\n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking if things are actually preventing me from doing what I need to do. Yeah, like I said, I'm getting things done, but it's definitely causing friction with my wife and the guys at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, she hasn't threatened to leave or anything drastic like that. And work... I mean, we haven't lost any big clients or had a total disaster. People have actually started giving me these looks, like they think I'm trying to rush them along or wrap up the conversation, even when I'm genuinely trying my best to just sit there and listen to them. I just can't seem to stay frozen in one spot.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these attention and restlessness issues started about a year ago. But thinking back to when you were younger—in school, or as a teenager—did you have similar problems with focusing or sitting still back then, or is this something that's pretty new to you?\n\nPatient: Well, looking back... yeah, it's definitely not new. I mean, I've always been like this, even as a kid. My teachers were constantly moving my seat because I talked too much or I'd just get up without asking. And I was always blurting out answers before they even finished the question. Actually, I remember my fifth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive beehive hairdo, like something straight out of the sixties. I don't know how she kept it up like that, probably went through a can of hairspray a week. She used to make me sit right next to her desk, which was right by the radiator, so I was always sweating. \n\nMy mom tried to talk to the principal about it once, because I kept coming home complaining about how hot it was. My mom was pretty intense when she wanted to be. She used to run this local bakery, made the best sourdough in town. She'd get up at like three in the morning every day to start the dough. I tried to help her out a few times when I was a teenager, but standing in one spot kneading bread for an hour? Forget about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the school thing... yeah, I've always had trouble sitting still and keeping my mouth shut. It's not just a recent thing.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, before fifth grade even, do you remember having these same kinds of problems—trouble paying attention in class or difficulty sitting still—or did those issues start later on?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, like I was saying, it goes way back. Even before fifth grade, I'm pretty sure. I mean, looking back, I was always the kid getting in trouble for that stuff. Teachers were always moving my seat, you know? Because I just talked too much, or I'd get up and wander around without asking, or I'd just blurt out the answer while they were right in the middle of asking the question. \n\nActually, speaking of blurting things out, I did that at a charity auction last month. It was for this local animal shelter—my wife's really into rescue dogs. We have three of them, all mutts. The biggest one, Buster, he's part Great Dane, part who-knows-what, and he eats like a horse. I'm buying those massive fifty-pound bags of kibble every other week, and I have to keep them in the garage because if I leave them in the pantry, he figures out how to open the door. He actually learned how to use the lever handle. Smart dog, but a total menace. \n\nAnyway, at the auction, they were taking bids on this vintage Fender Stratocaster, and before the guy even finished describing the year it was made, I just yelled out a bid that was way too high. Completely killed the bidding war. My wife was mortified, but hey, it went to a good cause, right? And it looks great in my office. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, it's always been there. Sitting still and keeping quiet just wasn't really my thing, even when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Beyond work, how has this difficulty focusing and the restlessness been affecting other parts of your life—things like your relationships, managing household responsibilities, or activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall, you know? The business is running, bills are paid. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like at home, my wife is getting really frustrated because we'll be talking about weekend plans or something, and I'll just zone out or start pacing, and she feels like I don't care. Which isn't true, I just can't focus on it. \n\nActually, speaking of weekend plans, we were supposed to go to this bed and breakfast up in the mountains last weekend. It's this place we went to for our anniversary five years ago, run by this older couple. The guy makes his own maple syrup—taps the trees right on the property. He showed me the whole process once, boiling the sap down in this huge sugar shack out back. It takes like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup, which is crazy when you think about it. I actually bought a couple of bottles to give out as client gifts, but then I ended up eating most of it myself on these protein pancakes I started making. I've been trying this new high-protein diet, you know, trying to build some muscle since I can't really do the heavy cardio anymore with my back. \n\nBut yeah, what was the question? Oh, how it affects things. Right. So yeah, it's costing me trust at home, and at work, people are definitely losing patience with me. Like when I'm trying to mentor some of the younger guys, or in those meetings I mentioned, things just don't go the way they should because I'm either cutting them off or jumping out of my seat. It's just... it's getting harder to manage, even though I'm technically still getting things done.\n\nDoctor: Back when you were in school—elementary, middle, or high school—did these attention and restlessness problems actually cause you to struggle academically, or get you into serious trouble, or did teachers and parents express real concern about it affecting your grades or your future?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about serious trouble, you know? Like, I wasn't failing out or anything. I mean, I dropped out eventually, but that was different. Before that, my grades were... fine, I guess. Nothing spectacular, but nobody was calling home saying I was ruining my life. \n\nActually, my history teacher in middle school, Mr. Davies, he was the one who really got me into World War II history. He had this whole collection of authentic medals and helmets he'd bring in. He let me try on an original M1 helmet once. Those things are heavy, you wouldn't believe it. The liner inside was all original too, smelled like old leather and dust. I actually started collecting a bit myself a few years ago. I found this incredible piece at an estate sale—a compass that supposedly belonged to a paratrooper in the 101st Airborne. The guy selling it had all these documents, but you never really know with that stuff, right? You have to get it authenticated, which is a whole process. There's this guy in Chicago I send pictures to, he's like the foremost expert on WWII field gear. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, school. Yeah, I don't remember anyone being super worried about my future or anything because of how I was acting. Just the usual telling me to sit down and be quiet.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, did your teachers ever hold you back a grade, or did you ever need to repeat a class, or get put in a special classroom for kids who were struggling academically?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I never got held back or put in any special classes or anything. I mean, I was always in the regular classes with everyone else. \n\nActually, speaking of regular classes, I remember my fifth-grade classroom had this huge terrarium in the back. Our teacher, Mrs. Gable, she kept a bearded dragon in there. His name was Spike. I used to volunteer to feed him crickets during recess because, honestly, I just liked watching him hunt. Crickets are surprisingly fast, but Spike had this way of just sitting perfectly still until they got close enough, and then—bam. Gone. I actually thought about getting a reptile for my office a while back, maybe a chameleon. They're fascinating, the way their eyes move independently. But my wife said absolutely not, she draws the line at the dogs. Plus, I don't think my assistant would be thrilled about keeping live insects in the breakroom fridge. She already complains about the smell of my protein shakes if I leave the shaker bottle in the sink too long. \n\nBut yeah, no special classes or repeating grades for me. Just regular school until I left.\n\nDoctor: Were you ever suspended or expelled from school, or did you ever have to go to summer school or take any special classes to catch up academically?\n\nPatient: Suspended? No, never. Never expelled either. And I definitely didn't do summer school. I couldn't have handled sitting in a classroom during the summer anyway. \n\nActually, summer was always my favorite time because my dad would take me out to his buddy's auto shop. That's where I first learned how to rebuild an engine. His friend had this 1969 Mustang Mach 1—candy apple red. Gorgeous car. We spent an entire July just stripping down the carburetor and putting it back together. I still love the smell of motor oil and gasoline, you know? It's weird, but it's comforting. I was actually looking at classic cars online the other night instead of sleeping. Found a pristine '72 Chevelle SS down in Texas, but the guy wants way too much for it. People think just because it has matching numbers they can charge whatever they want, but the market for classic muscle cars is actually softening a bit right now. \n\nBut anyway... what did you ask? Oh, right, catching up academically. No, I never had to take any special classes or anything like that. Just went along normally until I decided I'd had enough of school.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you dropped out in eleventh grade. Before that happened, were you actually struggling to keep up with the schoolwork itself, or was it more that you just didn't want to be there?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I'm not really sure if it was one or the other. I don't remember the work being impossible or anything, but maybe I just didn't care enough to find out? I just knew I didn't want to be sitting at a desk all day. I wanted to be out actually doing things, making money. \n\nRight after I left, I got a job installing car stereos at this local place off Route 9. Audio Express, I think it was called. Man, the things people would ask us to do to their cars... We had this one guy bring in a completely beat-up '98 Civic, and he wanted two massive 12-inch subwoofers crammed into the trunk. We had to use so much of that sound-deadening mat to keep the trunk lid from rattling right off the hinges, and even then, his license plate just vibrated like crazy. I learned a ton about electrical wiring there, though. You have to be super careful running the main power wire from the battery through the firewall, because if it rubs against the bare metal and shorts out... well, you're gonna have a fire on your hands. \n\nI actually still install my own audio equipment in my cars today. I just put a custom system in my truck last weekend. Took me like six hours, but the sound quality is just unbelievable compared to the factory speakers. The dealership always tries to sell you on their premium audio packages, but it's such a rip-off for what you actually get... \n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about eleventh grade?\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, do you remember if you were actually able to understand the lessons and do the work okay, or did you find the schoolwork itself confusing or hard to learn?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember it being hard to learn, honestly. I think I understood things fine when I actually paid attention. The problem was just sitting there while the teacher droned on and on. \n\nI actually had this one teacher in third grade, Mr. Peterson. He used to bring his guitar to class and play songs to help us remember multiplication tables. He had this vintage Martin acoustic, absolutely beautiful instrument. The resonance on those old Martins is just incredible, the wood ages in a way that gives it such a warm tone. I tried picking up guitar a few years ago, bought myself a nice Taylor, thinking I'd just learn a few chords to unwind after work. But man, building up those calluses on your fingers is brutal. And trying to find the time to practice consistently when you're running a company? Forget about it. I ended up giving the guitar to my nephew for his birthday. He's actually getting pretty good, started a little garage band with his friends. They play mostly 90s grunge covers, which I think is hilarious because they weren't even born when Nirvana was big. It's funny how music cycles back around like that, isn't it? Like vinyl records making a comeback. I saw a turntable at Best Buy the other day for like five hundred bucks...\n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Elementary school work? Yeah, I don't remember it being particularly confusing or anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned things are getting harder to manage at work and at home with your wife. Looking back over the past year or so, would you say these attention and restlessness problems have actually prevented you from doing things you needed to do, or have they mostly been annoying but you've still been able to get things done?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still keeping up overall. The business is running, things are getting done. But it's... it's definitely starting to cost me. Like, my wife is losing trust in me because family plans just don't go the way they should, you know? And at work, people are losing their patience with me during meetings or when I'm supposed to be mentoring the younger guys. \n\nSpeaking of mentoring, we just hired this kid straight out of college, engineering degree. Smart kid, but he doesn't know the first thing about actual practical application. He tried to redesign one of our standard manufacturing processes on his second day. I had to sit him down and explain that just because something looks more efficient on CAD software doesn't mean it translates to the factory floor. The software doesn't account for the fact that the forklift needs a wider turning radius when it's fully loaded, or that the hydraulic presses have a ten-second reset time. I actually love those old hydraulic presses we have. They're these massive, hulking machines from the 80s, built like absolute tanks. We had a guy from the manufacturer come out to service one last month, and he said they don't even make the replacement parts anymore. We have to custom machine our own seals for the pistons now. I actually went down to the shop floor and helped the machinist turn a new seal on the lathe. There's something really satisfying about watching metal shavings peel off a block of steel, you know? It's almost hypnotic. I could watch a lathe run all day. I actually watched like three hours of machining videos on YouTube last night...\n\nWait, sorry. Did I answer your question? You were asking if things are actually preventing me from doing what I need to do. Yeah, like I said, I'm getting things done, but it's definitely causing friction with my wife and the guys at work.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your wife is frustrated and people at work are losing patience with you. But concretely—have you actually missed deadlines, lost clients, had projects fail, or has your wife threatened to leave because of this? Or is it more that people are annoyed but everything is still basically working out?\n\nPatient: Well, no, she hasn't threatened to leave or anything drastic like that. And work... I mean, we haven't lost any big clients or had a total disaster. Like I said, I'm still keeping up overall, getting the important stuff done. But it's just... it's the little things that are adding up. It's costing me her trust when I say I'll handle something for the house and then completely forget, or when family plans go sideways because I can't just sit still through dinner. And at work, it's the patience of the guys in the shop, or when I'm trying to mentor someone and I just start rambling. \n\nLike that kid I was telling you about, the engineer. I was trying to explain our supply chain to him the other day, and we use this logistics company out of Chicago. Great guys, actually. The owner, Mike, he's a huge Bears fan. We went to a game at Soldier Field a couple years ago when they were playing the Packers. Freezing cold, middle of December, you know how the wind comes right off Lake Michigan? It just cuts right through you. I had on this heated jacket I bought online—best investment I ever made, seriously. It runs off these little lithium-ion power banks. You can actually use the same batteries to charge your phone if you're in a pinch. I take them camping with me now. We have this spot up in the mountains, total dead zone for cell service, which is honestly exactly what I need sometimes. Just me, the dog, and a campfire. I tried making that cowboy coffee last time, where you just boil the grounds right in the water? It was terrible. Absolutely gritty. I don't know how they drank that stuff back in the day. I ended up just buying one of those portable AeroPress things...\n\nWait, sorry. Did I actually answer you? No, nobody's quitting or leaving me, it's just... I'm burning through people's patience, I guess you could say."
